Least p-Variances Theory Thanks: 1 Research Fellow of the Alexander von Humboldt Foundation, Germany, (mmjamei@gmail.com). Thanks: 2 Faculty of Mathematics, K.N.Toosi University of Technology, Iran, (mmjamei@kntu.ac.ir).
by Mohammad Masjed-Jamei 1,2

Abstract
. As a result of a rather long-time research started in 2016, this theory whose structure is based on a fixed variable and an algebraic inequality, improves and somehow generalizes the well-known least squares theory. In fact, the fixed variable has a fundamental role in constituting the least p-variances theory. In this sense, some new concepts such as p-covariances with respect to a fixed variable, p-correlation coefficient with respect to a fixed variable and p-uncorrelatedness with respect to a fixed variable are first defined in order to establish least p-variance approximations. Then, we obtain a specific system called p-covariances linear system and apply the p-uncorrelatedness condition on its elements to find a general representation for p-uncorrelated variables. Afterwards, we apply the concept of p-uncorrelatedness for continuous functions particularly for polynomial sequences and find some new sequences such as a generic two-parameter hypergeometric polynomial of F 3 4 {}_{4}{F_{3}} type that satisfy such a p-uncorrelatedness property. In the sequel, we obtain an upper bound for 1-covariances, an approximation for p-variances, an improvement for the approximate solutions of over-determined systems and an improvement for the Bessel inequality and Parseval identity. Finally, we generalize the notion of least p-variance approximations based on several fixed orthogonal variables. For a better comprehension, here we bring a list of the main contents of this theory including 15 sections.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 93E24, 62J10, 42C05, Secondary: 33C47, 65F25.

1.
Introduction

Least squares theory is known as an essential tool for optimal approximations and regression analysis and has found extensive applications in mathematics, statistics, physics and engineering
[
6
,
9
,
25
,
30
]
. Some important parts of this theory include: best linear unbiased estimator, Gauss-Markov theorem, moving least squares method and its improvements
[
12
,
28
]
, Least-squares spectral analysis, orthogonal projections and least squares function and polynomial approximations
[
7
]
.

It was officially discovered and published by A. M. Legendre
[
13
]
in 1805 though it is also co-credited to C. F. Gauss who contributed significant theoretical advances to the method and may have previously used it in his work in 1795
[
29
]
.

The base of this theory is to minimize the sum of the squares of the residuals, which are the difference between observed values and the fitted values provided by an approximate linear (or nonlinear) model.

In many linear models
[
27
]
, in order to reduce the influence of errors in the derived observations, one would like to use a greater number of samplings than the number of unknown parameters in the model, which leads to the so called overdetermined linear systems. In other words, let
b\in{{\bf{R}}^{m}}
be a given vector and
A\in{{\bf{R}}^{m\times n}}
for
m>n
a given matrix. The problem is to find a vector
x\in{{\bf{R}}^{n}}
such that
Ax
is the best approximation to
b
.

There are many possible ways of defining the best solution, see
[
2
]
. A very common choice is to let
x
be a solution of the minimization problem

\mathop{\min}\limits_{x}\,\,\left\|{Ax-b\,}\right\|_{2}^{2},

where
\parallel.\parallel_{2}
denotes the Euclidean vector norm. Now, if one refers to
r=b-Ax
as the residual vector, a least squares solution in fact minimizes
\left\|{r\,}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}{r_{i}^{2}}
.

In linear statistical models, one assumes that the vector
b\in{{\bf{R}}^{m}}
of observations is related to the unknown parameters vector
x\in{{\bf{R}}^{n}}
by a linear relation

(1.1)

Ax=b+e,

where
A\in{{\bf{R}}^{m\times n}}
is a predetermined matrix of rank
n
and
e
denotes a vector of random errors. In the standard linear model, the following conditions are known as the basic conditions of Gauss-Markov theorem:

(1.2)

E(e)=0\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\rm{and}}\,\,\,\,\,\,{\mathop{\rm var}}(e)={\sigma^{2}}{I_{n}},

i.e., the random errors
e_{i}
are uncorrelated and all have zero means with the same variance in which
{\sigma^{2}}
is the true error variance.

In summary, the Gauss-Markov theorem expresses that if the linear model (
1.1
) is available, where
e
is a random vector with mean and variance given by (
1.2
), then the optimal solution of (
1.1
) is the least squares estimator, obtained by minimizing the sum of squares
\,\left\|{Ax-b-e\,}\right\|\,_{2}^{2}
. Furthermore,
E({s^{2}})={\sigma^{2}}
, where

{s^{2}}=\frac{1}{{m-n}}\left\|{b-Ax-Ae\,}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Similar to other available approximate techniques, least squares method contains some constrains and limitations. For instance, in the above mentioned theorem, the conditions (
1.2
) are necessary.

In this research, we introduce a new fixed variable in the minimization problem to somehow miniaturize the primitive quantity associated with sampling errors. Let us begin with a basic identity which gives rise to an important algebraic inequality, too.

Let
{\bf{S}}
denote an inner product space. Knowing that the elements of
{\bf{S}}
satisfy four properties of linearity, symmetry, homogeneity and positivity, there is an identity in this space which is directly related to the definition of the projection of two arbitrary elements of
{\bf{S}}
, i.e.

(1.3)

{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,{x_{2}}}}{x_{1}}\,=\frac{{\left\langle{{x_{1}},{x_{2}}}\right\rangle}}{{\left\langle{{x_{2}},{x_{2}}}\right\rangle}}\,\,{x_{2}}\,,

where
\left\langle{{x_{1}},{x_{2}}}\right\rangle
indicates the inner product of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
and
\left\langle{{x_{2}},{x_{2}}}\right\rangle=\left\|{{x_{2}}}\right\|{\,{}^{2}}\,\geq 0
denotes the norm square value of
x_{2}
. In other words, suppose
x,y,z
are three specific elements of
{\bf{S}}
and
p\in[0,1]
is a free parameter. Noting (
1.3
), the following identity holds true

(1.4)

\left\langle{x-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,z}}x\,,\,\,y-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,z}}y}\right\rangle=\left\langle{x,y}\right\rangle-p\frac{{\left\langle{x,z}\right\rangle\left\langle{y,z}\right\rangle}}{{\left\langle{z,z}\right\rangle}},

and for
y=x
it leads to the Schwarz inequality

(1.5)

\left\langle{x,x}\right\rangle-p\frac{{{{\left\langle{x,z}\right\rangle}^{2}}}}{{\left\langle{z,z}\right\rangle}}\geq 0\,,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall p\in[0,1].

This identity can also be presented in mathematical statistics. Essentially as the content of this research is about the least variance of an approximation based on a fixed variable, if we equivalently use the expected value symbol
E(.)
instead of the inner product symbol
\left\langle.\right\rangle
, though there is no difference between them for use, we will get to a new definition of covariance notion as follows:

Definition 1.1
.

p-Covariances with respect to a fixed variable

Let
X,Y
and
Z
be three random variables and
p\in[0,1]
. With reference to (
1.4
) we correspondingly define

(1.6)

\displaystyle{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}(X,Y;Z)

\displaystyle=E\left({\left({X-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E(XZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)\left({Y-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E(YZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=E(XY)-p\frac{{E(XZ)E(YZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,,

and call it ”p-covariance of
X
and
Y
with respect to the fixed variable
Z
”.

Note in (
1.6
) that

\frac{{E(XZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z={\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,Z}}X\,,

and therefore e.g. for
p=1
,

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{1}}(X,Y;Z)=E\Big({\left({X-\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,Z}}X}\right)\left({Y-\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,Z}}Y}\right)}\Big).

If
p=1
and
Z
follows a uniform distribution, say
Z=c^{*}
where
c^{*}\neq 0
is a constant value, then (
1.6
) will reduce to an ordinary covariance as

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{1}}\,(X,Y;c^{*})=E(XY)-E(X)E(Y)={\mathop{\rm cov}}\,(X,Y)\,.

Also for
p=0
, it follows that the fixed variable
Z
has no effect on definition (
1.6
).

For
Y=X
in (
1.6
), an extended definition of the ordinary variance is concluded as

(1.7)

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(X;Z)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}(X,X;Z)=E({X^{2}})-p\frac{{{E^{2}}(XZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,\geq 0\,,

where
E({Z^{2}})
is to be always positive.

Moreover, for any fixed variable
Z
and
p\in[0,1]
,

(1.8)

0\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\,(X;Z)\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(X;Z)\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{0}}\,(X;Z)=E({X^{2}}),

which is equivalent to

0\leq\left\langle{x-\frac{{\left\langle{x,z}\right\rangle}}{{\left\langle{z,z}\right\rangle}}\,\,z\,,\,\,x-\frac{{\left\langle{x,z}\right\rangle}}{{\left\langle{z,z}\right\rangle}}\,\,z}\right\rangle\\
\,\,\,\,\leq\left\langle{x-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{\left\langle{x,z}\right\rangle}}{{\left\langle{z,z}\right\rangle}}\,\,z\,,\,\,x-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{\left\langle{x,z}\right\rangle}}{{\left\langle{z,z}\right\rangle}}\,\,z}\right\rangle\leq\left\langle{x,x}\right\rangle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\forall z\in\mathbf{S},

in an inner product space. Note after simplification, the second part of the latter inequality is in the same form as (
1.5
).

Although inequality (
1.8
) shows that the best option is
p=1
, we prefer to apply the parametric case
p\in[0,1]
throughout this paper. The reasons for making such a decision will be revealed in forthcoming sections (see Figure 1 and the illustrative example
2.4
in this regard).

The following properties hold true for definitions (
1.6
) and (
1.7
):

a1)
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}(X,Y;Z)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}(Y,X;Z).

a2)
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(\alpha X,\beta Y;Z)=\alpha\beta\,\,{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}(X,Y;Z)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,(\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}).

a3)
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(X+\alpha,Y+\beta;Z)=

\qquad\qquad{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(X,Y;Z)+\alpha\,{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(1,Y;Z)+\beta\,\,{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(X,1;Z)+\alpha\beta\,\,{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(1,1;Z).

a4)
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(X;Z)=0\,\,{\rm{if}}\,p=1\,\,{\text{and}}\,\,Z=c^{*}X\,\,(c^{*}\neq 0).

a5)
\qquad{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(X,Y;c^{*}X)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(X,Y;c^{*}Y)=(1-p)E(XY)\,\,\,\,\,\,(c^{*}\neq 0).

a6)
\quad{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{c_{k}}{X_{k}}},{X_{m}};Z}\right)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{c_{k}}{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},{X_{m}};Z)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,(\{{c_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}).

and

(1.9)

{\rm{var}}_{p}(\alpha X+\beta Y;Z)={\alpha^{2}}\,{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(X;Z)+{\beta^{2}}{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(Y;Z)+2\alpha\beta\,\,{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(X,Y;Z).

Definition 1.2
.

p-Correlation coefficient with respect to a fixed variable

Based on relations (
1.6
) and (
1.7
), we can define a generalization of the Pearson correlation coefficient as

(1.10)

{\rho_{p}}\,(X,Y;Z)=\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,(X,Y;Z)}}{{\sqrt{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,(X;Z){\rm var}_{p}(Y;Z)}}},

and call it ”p-correlation coefficient of
X
and
Y
with respect to the fixed variable
Z
”.

It is straightforward to observe that

{\rho_{p}}(X,Y;Z)\in[-1,1],

because if the values

U=X-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E(XZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\rm{and}}\,\,\,\,\,\,V=Y-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E(YZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z,

are replaced in the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
[
18
]

(1.11)

{E^{2}}(UV)\leq E({U^{2}})E({V^{2}}),

then

{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}(X,Y;Z)\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(X;Z)\,\,{\rm var}_{p}(Y;Z).

In this sense, note that

E(UZ)=\sqrt{1-p}\,E(XZ)\,\,\,\,\,\,{\rm{and}}\,\,\,\,\,E(VZ)=\sqrt{1-p}\,E(YZ),

which are equal to zero only for the case
p=1
.

Definition 1.2.1.

p-Normal standard variable with respect to a fixed variable

Noting the definitions (
1.10
) and (
1.6
), since

{\rho_{p}}\,(X,Y;Z)=E\left({\left({\frac{{X-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,Z}}X}}{{\sqrt{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,(X;Z)}}}}\right)\left({\frac{{Y-(1-\sqrt{1-p}){\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,Z}}Y}}{{\sqrt{{\rm var}_{p}(Y;Z)}}}}\right)}\right),

a p-Normal standard variable, say
{N_{p}}(X;Z)
can be defined with respect to the fixed variable
Z
as

{N_{p}}(X;Z)=\frac{{X-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,Z}}X}}{{\sqrt{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,(X;Z)}}}.

For instance, we have

{N_{1}}(X;Z=c^{*})=\frac{{X-E(X)}}{{\sqrt{{\mathop{\rm var}}\,(X)}}}.

Definition 1.3
.

p-Uncorrelatedness with respect to a fixed variable

If in (
1.10
)
{\rho_{p}}(X,Y;Z)=0
, which is equivalent to the condition

p\,E(XZ)E(YZ)=E(XY)E({Z^{2}}),

then we say that
X
and
Y
are p-uncorrelated with respect to the fixed variable
Z
.

Such a definition can be expressed in an inner product space, too. We say that two elements
x,y\in{\bf{S}}
are p-uncorrelated with respect to the fixed element
z\in{\bf{S}}
if

(1.12)

\left({x-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\,\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{z}}x}\right)\,\bot\,\,\left({y-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\,\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{z}}y}\right),

or equivalently

p\,\left\langle{x,z}\right\rangle\left\langle{y,z}\right\rangle=\left\langle{x,y}\right\rangle\left\langle{z,z}\right\rangle.

As (
1.12
) shows,
p=0
gives rise to the well-known orthogonality property. In summary, 0-uncorrelatedness gives the orthogonality notion,
p=1
results in a complete uncorrelatedness and
p\in(0,1)
leads to an incomplete uncorrelatedness. In this paper, we apply the phrase ”complete uncorrelated” instead of 1-uncorrelated.

The aforesaid definition can similarly be expressed in a probability space. We say that two events
A
and
B
are p-independent with respect to the event
C
if

p\,{P_{r}}(\left.{A\,}\right|C)\,{P_{r}}(\left.{B\,}\right|C)={P_{r}}(A\cap B)\,,

which is equivalent to

p\,{P_{r}}(A\cap C){P_{r}}(B\cap C)={P_{r}}(A\cap B)P_{r}^{2}(C).

Hence, e.g. 1-independent means a complete independent with respect to the event
C
.

2.
Least p-variance approximations based on a fixed variable

Let
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
and
Y
be arbitrary random variables and consider the following approximation in the sequel

(2.1)

Y\cong\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{c_{k}}{X_{k}}}\,,

in which
\{{c_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
are unknown coefficients to be appropriately determined.

According to (
1.7
), the p-variance of the remaining term

(2.2)

R({c_{0}},{c_{1}},...,{c_{n}})=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{c_{k}}{X_{k}}}-Y\,,

is defined with respect to the fixed variable
Z
as

(2.3)

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z}\right)=E\left({{{\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}})-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E(Z\,R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}}))}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)}^{2}}}\right)\\
=E\left({{{\left({\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{c_{k}}\left({{X_{k}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E(Z{X_{k}})}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}Z}\right)}-(Y\,-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E(ZY)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}Z)}\right)}^{2}}}\right),

where
\frac{{E(Z\,R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}}))}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z
shows the projection of the error term (
2.2
) on the fixed variable
Z
and
\frac{{E(Z{X_{k}})}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}Z
the projection of each elements on
Z
. For the special case
p=1
, we have in fact considered (
2.3
) as

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\,\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z}\right)=E\left({{{\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}})-{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{z}}R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}})}\right)}^{2}}}\right).

We wish to find the unknown coefficients
\{{c_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
in (
2.3
) such that
{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z}\right)
is minimized. In this direction, it is important to point out that, according to inequality (
1.8
), the following inequalities always hold for any arbitrary variable
Z
:

(2.4)

0\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\,\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z}\right)\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z}\right)\\
\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{0}}\,\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z}\right)=E\left({{R^{2}}({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}})}\right).

This means that inequalities (
2.4
) are valid for any free selection of
\{{c_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
especially when they minimize the quantity (
2.3
). In other words, we have

\mathop{\min}\limits_{{c_{0}},...,{c_{n}}}{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});\,\,Z}\right)\leq\mathop{\min}\limits_{{c_{0}},...,{c_{n}}}{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});\,\,Z}\right)\leq\mathop{\min}\limits_{{c_{0}},...,{c_{n}}}E\left({{R^{2}}({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}})}\right),

which shows the superiority of the present theory with respect to the ordinary least squares theory (see examples
2.4
and
13.1
in this regard).

To minimize (
2.3
), for every
j=0,1,\,...\,,\,n
we have

(2.5)

\frac{{\partial{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,(R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z)}}{{\partial{c_{j}}}}=0\,\,\,\Rightarrow\\
2E\left(({X_{j}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}Z)\right.\\
\left.\left({\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{c_{k}}\left({{X_{k}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E({X_{k}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}Z}\right)}-(Y\,-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E(YZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}Z)}\right)\right)=0,

leading to the linear system

\displaystyle\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}},{X_{0}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{X_{0}};Z)}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},{X_{1}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{X_{1}};Z)}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}\cdots\\
\cdots\\
\vdots\\
\cdots\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},{X_{n}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}},{X_{n}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}};Z)}\end{array}}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}}\\
{{c_{1}}}\\
\vdots\\
{{c_{n}}}\end{array}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},Y;Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}},Y;Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},Y;Z)}\end{array}}\right].

Only finding the analytical solution of the above system is sufficient to guarantee that
{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z}\right)
is minimized, because we automatically have

\left\{{\frac{{{\partial^{2}}{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,(R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z)}}{{\partial{c_{j}}^{2}}}}\right\}_{j=0}^{n}\geq 0.

For instance, after solving (2.6) for
n=1
, the approximation (
2.1
) takes the form

Y\cong\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},Y;Z)-{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},{X_{1}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}};Z)-{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}\,({X_{0}},{X_{1}};Z)}}\,{X_{0}}\\
+\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}},Y;Z)-{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},{X_{1}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}};Z)-{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}\,({X_{0}},{X_{1}};Z)}}\,{X_{1}}\,.

In general, two continuous and discrete cases can be considered for the system (
2
), which we call it from now on ”p-covariances linear system with respect to the fixed variable
Z
”.

2.1.
First case of p-covariances linear system

If
\left\{{{X_{k}}={\Phi_{k}}(x)}\right\}_{k=0}^{n}
,
Y=f(x)
and
Z=z(x)
are defined in a continuous space with a probability density function as

{P_{r}}\left({X=x}\right)=\frac{{w(x)}}{{\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,dx}}},

for any arbitrary function
w(x)
positive on the interval
[a,b]
, then the components of the linear system (
2
) appear as

(2.30)

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({{\Phi_{i}}(x),{\Phi_{j}}(x)\,;z(x)}\right)=\\
\frac{1}{{\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,dx}}}\left({\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{i}}(x)\,{\Phi_{j}}(x)\,dx}-p\frac{{\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{i}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{j}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}}}{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}}}\right),

where
\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}>0
.

2.2.
Second case of p-covariances linear system

If the above-mentioned variables are defined on a counter set, say
{A^{*}}=\{{x_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{m}
with a discrete probability density function as

{P_{r}}\left({X=x}\right)=\frac{{j(x)}}{{\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x)}}},

for any arbitrary function
j(x)
positive on
x\in{A^{*}}
, then

(2.31)

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({{\Phi_{i}}(x),{\Phi_{j}}(x)\,;z(x)}\right)=\\
\frac{1}{{\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x)}}}\left({\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x){\Phi_{i}}(x)\,{\Phi_{j}}(x)}-p\frac{{\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x){\Phi_{i}}(x)\,z(x)}\,\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x){\Phi_{j}}(x)\,z(x)}}}{{\,\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)}}}}\right),

where
\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)}>0
.

One of the most important cases of the approximation (
2.1
) is when
\left\{{{X_{k}}={\Phi_{k}}(x)={x^{k}}}\right\}_{k=0}^{n}
having a uniform distribution function, which leads to the Hilbert problem
[
2
,
26
]
in a continuous space and to the polynomial type regression in a discrete space. In other words, if
\left\{{{X_{k}}={x^{k}}}\right\}_{k=0}^{n}
,
Y=f(x)
,
Z=z(x)
and
w(x)=1
for
x\in[0,1]
are substituted into (
2
), then

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({x^{i}},{x^{j}};z(x))=\frac{1}{{i+j+1}}-p\frac{{\int_{0}^{1}{\,{x^{i}}z(x)\,dx}\,\int_{0}^{1}{\,{x^{j}}z(x)\,dx}}}{{\int_{0}^{1}{\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\rm for}\,\,\,i,j=0,1,...,n,

and

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({x^{j}},f(x);\,z(x))=\int_{0}^{1}{\,{x^{j}}f(x)\,dx}-p\frac{{\int_{0}^{1}{\,{x^{j}}z(x)\,dx}\,\int_{0}^{1}{\,f(x)z(x)\,dx}}}{{\int_{0}^{1}{\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}}\,\,\,\,\,{\rm for}\,\,j=0,1,...,n.

For
p=0
, we clearly encounter with the Hilbert problem.

Similarly, if in a discrete space
\left\{{{X_{k}}={x^{k}}}\right\}_{k=0}^{n}
,
{A^{*}}=\{{x_{k}}\}_{k=1}^{m}
,
Y=f(x)
where
f({x_{k}})={y_{k}}
,
Z=z(x)
and
j(x)=1
, the entries of the system (
2
) respectively take the forms

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({x^{i}},{x^{j}};z(x))=\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{{({x_{k}})}^{i+j}}}\,-\frac{p}{m}\frac{{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{{({x_{k}})}^{i}}z({x_{k}})}\,\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{{({x_{k}})}^{j}}z({x_{k}})}}}{{\,\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{z^{2}}({x_{k}})}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,{\rm for}\,\,\,i,j=0,1,...,n,

and

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({x^{j}},f(x);\,z(x))=\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{y_{k}}{{({x_{k}})}^{j}}}\,-\frac{p}{m}\frac{{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{{({x_{k}})}^{j}}z({x_{k}})}\,\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{y_{k}}\,z({x_{k}})}}}{{\,\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{z^{2}}({x_{k}})}}}\,\,{\rm for}\,\,j=0,1,...,n.

As a sample, the system corresponding to the linear regression
Y={c_{0}}{X_{0}}+{c_{1}}{X_{1}}={c_{0}}+{c_{1}}x
with respect to the fixed variable
Z=z(x)
appears as

\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{m\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{z^{2}}({x_{k}})}-p{{\big(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{z({x_{k}})}\big)^{2}}},}&{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{z^{2}}({x_{k}})}-p\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{z({x_{k}})}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}z({x_{k}})}}\\
{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{z^{2}}({x_{k}})}-p\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{z({x_{k}})}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}z({x_{k}})}\,,}&{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{x_{k}^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{z^{2}}({x_{k}})}-p{{\big(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}z({x_{k}})}\big)^{2}}}}\end{array}}\right]\\
\times\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}}\\
{{c_{1}}}\end{array}}\right]=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{y_{k}}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{z^{2}}({x_{k}})}-p\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{z({x_{k}})}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{y_{k}}z({x_{k}})}}\\
{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}{y_{k}}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{z^{2}}({x_{k}})}-p\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}z({x_{k}})}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{y_{k}}z({x_{k}})}}\end{array}}\right].

It is interesting to know that if
z(x)=c^{*}\neq 0
is substituted into the above partially system for
p\neq 1
, the output gives the well-known result

Y=\frac{{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{x_{k}^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{y_{k}}}-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}{y_{k}}}}}{{m\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{x_{k}^{2}}-{{(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}})}^{2}}}}+\frac{{m\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}{y_{k}}}-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{y_{k}}}}}{{m\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{x_{k}^{2}}-{{(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}})}^{2}}}}x\,,

i.e.
p\neq 1
has been automatically deleted. In this sense, if the only case
p=1
is substituted into the above mentioned system for

z(x)=c^{*}\neq 0
as

\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{0,}&0\\
{0\,,}&{m\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{x_{k}^{2}}-{{\big(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}}\big)^{2}}}}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}}\\
{{c_{1}}}\end{array}}\right]=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}0\\
{m\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}{y_{k}}}-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{y_{k}}}}\end{array}}\right],

the output gives the result

Y={c_{0}}+\frac{{m\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}{y_{k}}}-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{y_{k}}}}}{{m\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{x_{k}^{2}}-{{(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{{x_{k}}})}^{2}}}}x\,,

where
c_{0}
is a free parameter.

In the next section, we show that why
c_{0}
became a free parameter when
z(x)=c^{*}\neq 0
and
p=1
.

2.3.
Some reducible cases of the p-covariances linear system

2.3.1.
First case.

Suppose there exists a specific distribution for the fixed variable
Z
such that

(2.32)

E(Z{X_{k}})=0\qquad\text{for every}\qquad k=0,1,...,n.

Then, the p-covariances system (
2
) reduces to the well-known normal equations system
[
2
]

(2.33)

\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{E\,(X_{0}^{2})}\\
{E({X_{1}}{X_{0}})}\\
\vdots\\
{E({X_{n}}{X_{0}})}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{E({X_{0}}{X_{1}})}\\
{E\,(X_{1}^{2})}\\
\vdots\\
{E({X_{n}}{X_{1}})}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}\cdots\\
\cdots\\
\vdots\\
\cdots\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{E({X_{0}}{X_{n}})}\\
{E({X_{1}}{X_{n}})}\\
\vdots\\
{E\,(X_{n}^{2})}\end{array}}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}}\\
{{c_{1}}}\\
\vdots\\
{{c_{n}}}\end{array}}\right]=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{E({X_{0}}Y)}\\
{E({X_{1}}Y)}\\
\vdots\\
{E({X_{n}}Y)}\end{array}}\right],

and

(2.34)

\mathop{\min}\limits_{{c_{0}},...,{c_{n}}}{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\Big({{R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});\,\,Z\,}\Big|\,\,\{E(Z{X_{k}})\}_{k=0}^{n}=0\,}\Big)=\mathop{\min}\limits_{{c_{0}},...,{c_{n}}}E\left({{R^{2}}({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}})}\right).

Also, it is obvious for
p=0
that

\mathop{\min}\limits_{{c_{0}},...,{c_{n}}}{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{0}}\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});\,\,Z}\right)=\mathop{\min}\limits_{{c_{0}},...,{c_{n}}}E\left({{R^{2}}({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}})}\right).

2.3.2.
Second case.

If
Z
follows a uniform distribution (say
Z=c^{*}\neq 0
) and
{X_{0}}=1
, the condition (
2.32
) is no longer valid for
k=0
because
c^{*}E(1)\neq 0
. Therefore, noting that
{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(X,Y;c^{*})=E(XY)-p\,E(X)E(Y)
, the linear system (
2
) reduces to

(2.35)

\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{1-p}\\
{(1-p)E({X_{1}})}\\
\vdots\\
{(1-p)E({X_{n}})}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{(1-p)E({X_{1}})}\\
{E\,(X_{1}^{2})-p\,{E^{2}}({X_{1}})}\\
\vdots\\
{E({X_{n}}{X_{1}})-pE({X_{n}})E({X_{1}})}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}\cdots\\
\cdots\\
\vdots\\
\cdots\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{(1-p)E({X_{n}})}\\
{E({X_{1}}{X_{n}})-pE({X_{1}})E({X_{n}})}\\
\vdots\\
{E\,(X_{n}^{2})-p{E^{2}}({X_{n}})}\end{array}}\end{array}}\right]\\
\times\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}}\\
{{c_{1}}}\\
\vdots\\
{{c_{n}}}\end{array}}\right]=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{(1-p)E(Y)}\\
{E({X_{1}}Y)-pE({X_{1}})E(Y)}\\
\vdots\\
{E({X_{n}}Y)-pE({X_{n}})E(Y)}\end{array}}\right],

Relation (
2.35
) shows that for
p=1
,
c_{0}
is a free parameter. For
{X_{0}}=1
, the approximation (
2.1
) takes the simplified form

Y\cong{c_{0}}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}{{c_{k}}{X_{k}}}\,,

and replacing it in the normal system (
2.33
) yields

(2.36)

\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}1\\
{E({X_{1}})}\\
\vdots\\
{E({X_{n}})}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{E({X_{1}})}\\
{E\,(X_{1}^{2})}\\
\vdots\\
{E({X_{n}}{X_{1}})}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}\cdots\\
\cdots\\
\vdots\\
\cdots\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{E({X_{n}})}\\
{E({X_{1}}{X_{n}})}\\
\vdots\\
{E\,(X_{n}^{2})}\end{array}}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}}\\
{{c_{1}}}\\
\vdots\\
{{c_{n}}}\end{array}}\right]=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{E(Y)}\\
{E({X_{1}}Y)}\\
\vdots\\
{E({X_{n}}Y)}\end{array}}\right].

Now, the important point is that after some elementary operations, the system (
2.36
) will exactly be transformed to the linear system (
2.35
). This means that for any arbitrary
p\in[0,1]
we have

(2.37)

\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathop{\min}\limits_{{c_{1}},...,{c_{n}}}{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\Big({{R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z\,}\Big|\,\,Z=c^{*}\,\,{\rm{and}}\,\,{X_{0}}=1}\Big)\\
=\mathop{\min}\limits_{{c_{1}},...,{c_{n}}}E\left({{R^{2}}({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}})\,\Big|{X_{0}}=1\,\,{\rm{and}}\,\,{c_{0}}=E(Y)-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}{{c_{k}}E({X_{k}})}}\right).

Such a result in (
2.37
) can similarly be proved for every
Z={X_{k}}
where
k=1,2,..,n
, as the following example approves it.

2.4.
An illustrative example and the role of the fixed variable in it

The previous sections
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
affirm that the fixed variable
Z
has a basic role in the theory of least p-variances. Here we present an illustrative example to show the importance of such a fixed function in constituting the initial approximation (
2.1
). Let
Y=\sqrt{1-x}
be defined on
[0,1]
together with the probability density function
w(x)=1
and the fixed function
z(x)={x^{\lambda}}
where
\lambda>-1/2
because
\int_{\,0}^{1}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}=\frac{1}{{2\lambda+1}}
. For the basis functions
\left\{{{X_{k}}={x^{k}}}\right\}_{k=0}^{2}
, the initial approximation (
2.1
) takes the form

(2.38)

\sqrt{1-x}\cong{c_{0}}(\lambda,p)+{c_{1}}(\lambda,p)\,x+{c_{2}}(\lambda,p)\,{x^{2}}={{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;\lambda,p),

in which the unknown coefficients satisfy the following linear system according to (
2
):

\displaystyle\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{1-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{{{(\lambda+1)}^{2}}}}p,}&{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{(\lambda+1)(\lambda+2)}}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{(\lambda+1)(\lambda+3)}}p}\\
{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{(\lambda+1)(\lambda+2)}}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{{{(\lambda+2)}^{2}}}}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{(\lambda+2)(\lambda+3)}}p}\\
{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{(\lambda+1)(\lambda+3)}}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{(\lambda+2)(\lambda+3)}}p,}&{\frac{1}{5}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{{{(\lambda+3)}^{2}}}}p}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}(\lambda,p)}\\
{{c_{1}}(\lambda,p)}\\
{{c_{2}}(\lambda,p)}\end{array}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\frac{2}{3}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{\lambda+1}}B(\lambda+1,\frac{3}{2})\,p}\\
{\frac{4}{{15}}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{\lambda+2}}B(\lambda+1,\frac{3}{2})\,p}\\
{\frac{{16}}{{105}}-\frac{{2\lambda+1}}{{\lambda+3}}B(\lambda+1,\frac{3}{2})\,p}\end{array}}\right],

where

B(a,b)=\int_{\,0}^{1}{{x^{a-1}}{{(1-x)}^{b-1}}dx}=\frac{{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}}{{\Gamma(a+b)}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,(a,b>0).

After solving (
2.4
),
{{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;\lambda,p)
will be determined and since the remaining term of (
2.38
) is defined as

{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;\lambda,p)={{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;\lambda,p)-\sqrt{1-x},

so

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\left({{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;\lambda,p);{x^{\lambda}}}\right)=E\left({{{\left({{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;\lambda,p)}\right)}^{2}}}\right)-p\,(2\lambda+1){E^{2}}\left({{x^{\lambda}}{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;\lambda,p)}\right).

However, there are two different cases for the parameter
\lambda>-1/2
which should be studied separately.

2.4.1.
First case.

If
\lambda=0,1,2
, then the fixed functions
z(x)=1,x,{x^{2}}
coincide respectively with the first, second and third basis functions in (
2.38
) and the system (
2.4
) is simplified for
\lambda=0
as

\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{1-p,}&{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{3}p}\\
{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{4}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{6}p}\\
{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{3}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{6}p,}&{\frac{1}{5}-\frac{1}{9}p}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}(0,p)}\\
{{c_{1}}(0,p)}\\
{{c_{2}}(0,p)}\end{array}}\right]=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\frac{2}{3}-\frac{2}{3}\,p}\\
{\frac{4}{{15}}-\frac{1}{3}p}\\
{\frac{{16}}{{105}}-\frac{2}{9}\,p}\end{array}}\right],

and for
\lambda=1
as

\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{1-\frac{3}{4}p,}&{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{3}{8}p}\\
{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{3}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4}p}\\
{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{3}{8}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4}p,}&{\frac{1}{5}-\frac{3}{{16}}p}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}(1,p)}\\
{{c_{1}}(1,p)}\\
{{c_{2}}(1,p)}\end{array}}\right]=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\frac{2}{3}-\frac{2}{5}\,p}\\
{\frac{4}{{15}}-\frac{4}{{15}}p}\\
{\frac{{16}}{{105}}-\frac{1}{5}\,p}\end{array}}\right],

and finally for
\lambda=2
as

\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{1-\frac{5}{9}p,}&{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{5}{{12}}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{3}p}\\
{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{5}{{12}}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{5}{{16}}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4}p}\\
{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{3}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4}p,}&{\frac{1}{5}-\frac{1}{5}p}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}(2,p)}\\
{{c_{1}}(2,p)}\\
{{c_{2}}(2,p)}\end{array}}\right]=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\frac{2}{3}-\frac{{16}}{{63}}\,p}\\
{\frac{4}{{15}}-\frac{4}{{21}}p}\\
{\frac{{16}}{{105}}-\frac{{16}}{{105}}\,p}\end{array}}\right].

According to the result (
2.37
), the solutions of all above systems must be the same and independent of
p
, so that in the final form we have

{{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;0,p)={{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;1,p)={{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;2,p)={{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;\lambda,0)=-\frac{4}{7}\,{x^{2}}-\frac{8}{{35}}x+\frac{{34}}{{35}}.

Note for
p=1
, after solving (
2.4
) we get

{{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;0,1)=-\frac{4}{7}\,{x^{2}}-\frac{8}{{35}}x+{c_{0}}(0,1),

in which
{c_{0}}(0,1)=E\left({\sqrt{1-x}}\right)+\frac{4}{7}\,E({x^{2}})+\frac{8}{{35}}E(x)=\frac{{34}}{{35}}
according to (
2.37
) again.

In this sense,

E\left({{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;0,1)}\right)=E\left({{{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;0,1)-\sqrt{1-x}}\right)=0,

implies that

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\left({{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;0,1);z(x)=1}\right)=E\left({{{\left({{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;0,1)}\right)}^{2}}}\right)\\
={\int_{\,0}^{1}{\left({-\frac{4}{7}\,{x^{2}}-\frac{8}{{35}}x+\frac{{34}}{{35}}-\sqrt{1-x}}\right)}^{2}}dx=\frac{1}{{2450}}\cong 0.000408.

2.4.2.
Second case.

Any assumption other than
\lambda=0,1,2
causes the system (
2.4
) to have a unique solution. For example,
\lambda=1/2
simplifies (
2.4
) in the form

(2.48)

\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{1-\frac{8}{9}p,}&{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{8}{{15}}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{8}{{21}}p}\\
{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}p,}&{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{8}{{25}}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{8}{{35}}p}\\
{\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{3}p,}&{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{8}{{35}}p,}&{\frac{1}{5}-\frac{8}{{49}}p}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{c_{0}}(\frac{1}{2},p)}\\
{{c_{1}}(\frac{1}{2},p)}\\
{{c_{2}}(\frac{1}{2},p)}\end{array}}\right]=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\frac{2}{3}-\frac{1}{6}\,\pi p}\\
{\frac{4}{{15}}-\frac{1}{{10}}\pi p}\\
{\frac{{16}}{{105}}-\frac{1}{{14}}\,\pi p}\end{array}}\right].

After solving the system (
2.48
) we obtain

{{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;\frac{1}{2},p)=-\frac{7}{3}\,\frac{{(75\pi+64)p-300}}{{1224p-1225}}{x^{2}}+20\frac{{(21\pi-80)p+14}}{{1224p-1225}}x+\frac{1}{2}\frac{{(105\pi+2048)p-2380}}{{1224p-1225}}.

For the special case
p=1
, i.e.

{{\bf{A}}_{2}}(x;\frac{1}{2},1)=-(\frac{{1652}}{3}-175\,\pi)\,{x^{2}}+(1320-420\,\pi)\,x+166-\frac{{105}}{2}\,\pi,

we have

E\left({{x^{\frac{1}{2}}}{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;\frac{1}{2},1)}\right)=-\frac{2}{7}(\frac{{1652}}{3}-175\,\pi)\,+\frac{2}{5}(1320-420\,\pi)\,+\frac{2}{3}(166-\frac{{105}}{2}\,\pi)-\frac{\pi}{8},

and consequently

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\left({{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;\frac{1}{2},1);{x^{\frac{1}{2}}}}\right)=E\left({{{\Big({{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;\frac{1}{2},1)\Big)^{2}}}}\right)-2{E^{2}}\left({{x^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;\frac{1}{2},1)}\right)\cong 0.000388.

As we observe

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\left({{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;\frac{1}{2},1);{x^{\frac{1}{2}}}}\right)<{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\left({{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;0,1);1}\right)=E\left({{{\left({{{\bf{R}}_{2}}(x;0,1)}\right)}^{2}}}\right),

which clearly shows the role of the fixed function in the obtained approximations.

2.5.
How to choose a suitable option for the fixed variable: A geometric interpretation

Although inequality (
2.4
) holds for any arbitrary variable
Z
, our wish is to determine some conditions to find a suitable option for the fixed variable
Z
such that

(2.49)

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\,(X;Z)\ll E({X^{2}}),

and/or

(2.50)

\frac{{{E^{2}}(XZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\gg 0\,.

Various states can be considered for the above-mentioned goal. For example, replacing
Z=c^{*}\neq 0
in (
2.50
) yields
{E^{2}}(X)\gg 0\,
which means if the magnitude of the mean value
E(X)\neq 0
is very big, we expect that the presented theory based on the fixed variable
Z=c^{*}\neq 0
acts much better than the ordinary least squares theory.

Another approach is to directly minimize the value
{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\,(X;Z)
in (
2.49
) such that

X-\frac{{E(XZ)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z\to 0\,.

The following figure describes our aim in a vector space.

Figure 1.
Least p-variances based on the fixed vector
\vec{Z}
and the role of
p
from 0 to 1

In this figure,
\left\|{\vec{X}}\right\|_{\,2}^{\,2}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}{x_{k}^{2}}
leads to the same as ordinary least squares for
p=0
,
\left\|{\vec{X}-\vec{A}}\right\|_{\,2}^{\,2}=\left\|{\vec{X}-{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,\vec{Z}}}\vec{X}\,}\right\|_{\,2}^{\,2}
leads to the complete type of least variances based on
\vec{Z}
and
p=1
and finally
\left\|{\vec{X}-p\,\vec{A}}\right\|_{\,2}^{\,2}
leads to the least p-variances based on
\vec{Z}
and
p\in[0,1]
.

3.
p-uncorrelatedness condition on the p-covariances linear system and its consequences

Since the coefficients matrix of the system (
2
) is symmetric and all its diagonal entries are positive, the aforesaid system is solvable and its unknown coefficients are computable. However, as we observed in the previous example, analytically solving such linear systems is difficult from computational point of view. To resolve this problem, we can apply the condition

(3.1)

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{i}},{X_{j}};Z)={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z)\,{\delta_{i,j}}\,\,\,{\rm{for}}\,{\rm{every}}\,\,\,i,j=0,1,...,n\,\,\,{\rm{with}}\,\,\,{\delta_{i,j}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}0\,\,\,\,\,(i\neq j),\\
1\,\,\,\,\,\,(i=j),\end{array}\right.

on the elements of the system (
2
) to easily obtain the unknown coefficients in the form

{c_{k}}=\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}.

In this case

(3.2)

Y\cong\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}\,,

would be the best approximation in the sense of least p-variance of the error with respect to the fixed variable
Z
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Any finite set of random variables satisfying the p-uncorrelatedness condition (
3.1
) is linearly independent.

Proof.

Assume that
\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{a_{k}}{X_{k}}}=0
where
\{{a_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
are not all zero and
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
are p-uncorrelated variables satisfying (
3.1
). Then, applying
{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(.\,,{X_{j}};Z)
on both sides of this assumption yields

0={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\left({\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{a_{k}}{X_{k}}},{X_{j}};Z}\right)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{a_{k}}{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},{X_{j}};Z)}={a_{j}}{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z),

which implies that
{a_{j}}=0
for
j=0,1,\ldots,n
, i.e. a contradiction.
∎

The question is now how to find linear combinations that are p-uncorrelated with respect to the variable
Z
. There is a basic theorem in this direction similar to the Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization theorem
[
5
]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
be a finite or infinite sequence of random variables such that any finite number of elements
\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
are linearly independent. One can find constants
\{{a_{i,j}}\}
such that the elements

(3.3)

\begin{array}[]{l}{X_{0}}={V_{0}},\\
{X_{1}}={V_{1}}+{a_{12}}{V_{0}},\\
{X_{2}}={V_{2}}+{a_{22}}{V_{1}}+{a_{23}}{V_{0}},\\
\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
{X_{n}}={V_{n}}+{a_{n2}}{V_{n-1}}+...+{a_{n,n+1}}{V_{0}},\end{array}

are mutually p-uncorrelated with respect to the fixed variable
Z

Proof.

Let us set recursively

(3.4)

\begin{array}[]{l}{X_{0}}={V_{0}},\\
{X_{1}}={V_{1}}-\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},{V_{1}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}};Z)}}{X_{0}},\\
\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
{X_{n}}={V_{n}}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},{V_{n}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}.\end{array}

As
X_{n}
is a linear combination of
\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
in (
3.4
), it is enough to show that
X_{n}
is p-uncorrelated to
\{{X_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{n-1}
with respect to the variable
Z
. For
j=0,1,\ldots,n-1
we have

\displaystyle{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{X_{j}};Z)

\displaystyle={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({{V_{n}}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},{V_{n}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}},{X_{j}};Z}\right)

\displaystyle={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({V_{n}},{X_{j}};Z)-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},{V_{n}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},{X_{j}};Z)}

\displaystyle={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({V_{n}},{X_{j}};Z)-{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}},{V_{n}};Z)=0.

∎

In fact, theorem
3.2
is a generalization of Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization process when
p=0
or the condition
E(Z{V_{k}})=0
holds for every
k=0,1,...,n
.

Theorem 3.3
.

The reverse proposition of the previous theorem is: There are constants
\{{b_{i,j}}\}
such that

(3.5)

\begin{array}[]{l}{V_{0}}={X_{0}},\\
{V_{1}}={X_{1}}+{b_{12}}{X_{0}},\\
{V_{2}}={X_{2}}+{b_{22}}{X_{1}}+{b_{23}}{X_{0}},\\
\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\vdots\\
{V_{n}}={X_{n}}+{b_{n2}}{X_{n-1}}+...+{b_{n,n+1}}{X_{0}},\end{array}

and

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{V_{k}};Z)=0\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\rm{for}}\,\,\,k=0,1,...,n-1,

provided that

(3.6)

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{i}},{X_{j}};Z)=0\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\rm{for}}\,\,\,\,\,i\neq j.

Proof.

By virtue of the fact that the system (
3.3
) is invertible, the general element of (
3.5
) in the form

{V_{k}}={X_{k}}+{b_{k2}}{X_{k-1}}+...+{b_{k,k+1}}{X_{0}},

gives

\displaystyle{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{V_{k}};Z)

\displaystyle={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{X_{k}}+\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k+1}{{b_{k,j}}{X_{k+1-j}}};Z)

\displaystyle={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{X_{k}};Z)+\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k+1}{{b_{k,j}}{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{X_{k+1-j}};Z)}=0,

which is valid for every
k=0,1,\ldots,n-1
according to the condition (
3.6
).
∎

3.1.
A general representation for p-uncorrelated variables

Both previous theorems
3.2
and
3.3
help us now obtain a general representation for p-uncorrelated variables with respect to the variable
Z
. Assume that
\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
is an arbitrary independent sequence of random variables and
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
satisfy the condition (
3.1
) as before. Noting the last row of (
3.4
) and this fact from (
3.3
) that
{X_{n}}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{a_{n,n+1-k}}\,{V_{k}}}
where
{a_{n,1}}=1
, for
m\leq n
we have

(3.7)

\displaystyle{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{X_{m}};Z)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\left({{X_{n}},{V_{m}}-\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m-1}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}},{V_{m}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z)}}{X_{j}}};Z}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}},{V_{m}};Z)-\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m-1}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}},{V_{m}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z)}}\,{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\left({{X_{n}},{X_{j}};Z}\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({V_{m}},{X_{n}};Z)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({V_{m}},\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{a_{n,n+1-k}}\,{V_{k}}};Z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{a_{n,n+1-k}}\,{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,\left({{V_{m}},{V_{k}};Z}\right)}={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}};Z)\,{\delta_{m,n}}.

For
m=0,1,...,n
, relation (
3.7
) leads eventually to the linear system

\displaystyle\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}},{V_{0}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{n}},{V_{0}};Z)}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{1}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{n}},{V_{1}};Z)}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}\cdots\\
\cdots\\
\vdots\\
\cdots\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{n}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}},{V_{n}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{n}};Z)}\end{array}}\end{array}}\right]\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{a_{n,n+1}}}\\
{{a_{n,n}}}\\
\vdots\\
{{a_{n,1}}}\end{array}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\left[{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}};Z)}\end{array}}\right].

If the determinant

(3.32)

\Delta_{n}^{(p)}\left({\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n};Z}\right)=\left|{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}},{V_{0}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{n}},{V_{0}};Z)}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{1}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{n}},{V_{1}};Z)}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}\cdots\\
\cdots\\
\vdots\\
\cdots\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{n}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}},{V_{n}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{n}};Z)}\end{array}}\end{array}}\right|,

is defined with
\Delta_{-1}^{(p)}(.)=1
, the first result of solving the system (
3.1
) is that the value
{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}};Z)
can be computed in terms of determinant (
3.32
) as

(3.33)

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,({X_{n}};Z)=\frac{{\Delta_{n}^{(p)}\left({\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n};Z}\right)}}{{\Delta_{n-1}^{(p)}\left({\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n-1};Z}\right)}}\,.

On the other side, it follows from (
3.33
) as a first order equation that

(3.34)

\Delta_{n}^{(p)}\left({\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n};Z}\right)=\prod\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}\,\,\geq 0\,.

The second result of solving (
3.1
) is to derive a general representation for
X_{n}
as

(3.35)

\Delta_{n-1}^{(p)}\left({\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n-1};Z}\right){X_{n}}\\
=\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}},{V_{0}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{n-1}},{V_{0}};Z)}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{1}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{n-1}},{V_{1}};Z)}\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}\cdots\\
\cdots\\
\vdots\\
\cdots\end{array}}&{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{n}};Z)}\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}},{V_{n}};Z)}\\
\vdots\\
{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{n-1}},{V_{n}};Z)}\end{array}}\end{array}\\
\qquad\qquad{V_{0}}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,{V_{1}}\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdots\qquad\qquad\qquad{V_{n}}\end{array}\right|\,,

where we have exploited (
3.33
) to derive it.

Inequality (
3.34
) shows that the determinant of the coefficients matrix of the system (
2
) is always non-negative. Moreover, if
p=0
or
E(Z{X_{k}})=0
for every
k=0,1,\ldots,n
in (
3.35
), it leads to the Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization process. For instance, the expanded forms of (
3.35
) for
n=0,1,2
are

\displaystyle{X_{0}}

\displaystyle={V_{0}},

\displaystyle{X_{1}}

\displaystyle={V_{1}}-\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{1}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}};Z)}}{V_{0}},

\displaystyle{X_{2}}

\displaystyle={V_{2}}-\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}},{V_{2}};Z)-{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{1}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{2}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}};Z)-{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}({V_{0}},{V_{1}};Z)}}\,\,{V_{1}}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,-\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{2}};Z)-{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}},{V_{1}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}},{V_{2}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{0}};Z){{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({V_{1}};Z)-{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}({V_{0}},{V_{1}};Z)}}\,\,{V_{0}}\,,

satisfying the conditions

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},{X_{1}};Z)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{0}},{X_{2}};Z)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{1}},{X_{2}};Z)=0.

Note although representation (
3.35
) is computationally slower than the recursive algorithm described in theorem
3.2
, it is however of theoretical interest as we have used it to find new sequences of p-uncorrelated functions (see examples of sections
6
,
7
,
8
and
9
).

4.
p-uncorrelated expansions with respect to a fixed variable

Let
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
be a sequence of p-uncorrelated variables with respect to the variable
Z
. If
n\rightarrow\infty
in approximation (
3.2
), the series

\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}},

is called a p-uncorrelated expansion with respect to
Z
for
Y
and then we write

(4.1)

Y\sim\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}\,.

In the case where
n
is finite, there is a separate theorem as follows.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
be linearly independent variables and
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
be their corresponding p-uncorrelated elements generated by
\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
in theorem
3.2
. If
\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{a_{k}}{V_{k}}}={W_{n}}
then

(4.2)

{W_{n}}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},{W_{n}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}\,.

Proof.

First from theorem
3.3
and relation (
3.5
), we have

(4.3)

\displaystyle{W_{n}}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{a_{k}}{V_{k}}}={a_{0}}({X_{0}})+{a_{1}}({X_{1}}+{b_{12}}{X_{0}})+...+{a_{n}}({X_{n}}+{b_{n2}}{X_{n-1}}+...+{b_{n,n+1}}{X_{0}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad={c_{0}}{X_{0}}+{c_{1}}{X_{1}}+...+{c_{n}}{X_{n}}.

Now, for
k=0,1,\ldots,n
apply
{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}(.\,,{X_{k}};Z)
on both sides of (
4.3
) to get

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}({W_{n}},{X_{k}};Z)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{{c_{j}}{X_{j}}},{X_{k}};Z)=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{{c_{j}}{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}},{X_{k}};Z)}={c_{k}}{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}({X_{k}};Z),

which yields

{c_{k}}=\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({W_{n}},{X_{k}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}},

and (
4.2
) is therefore proved.
∎

Remark
4.2
.

There is an important point in theorem
4.1
. Let us reconsider
n+1
mutually p-uncorrelated elements
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
satisfying the condition

(4.4)

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{i}},{X_{j}};Z)={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z)\,{\delta_{i,j}}.

Since

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{i}},{X_{j}};Z)=E\left({\left({{X_{i}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E({X_{i}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)\left({{X_{j}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)}\right),

by defining the variable

(4.5)

{T_{j,p}}(Z)={X_{j}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z={X_{j}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,Z}}{X_{j}},

we observe in (4.4) that

(4.6)

E\Big({{T_{i,p}}(Z){T_{j,p}}(Z)}\Big)=E\Big({T_{j,p}^{2}(Z)}\Big){\delta_{i,j}}.

Now notice that the orthogonal sequence
\left\{{{T_{j,p}}(Z)}\right\}_{j=0}^{n}
in (
4.5
) is generated only if we have previously created the uncorrelated sequence
\left\{{{X_{j}}}\right\}_{j=0}^{n}
because of the term
\frac{{E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z
otherwise generating such orthogonal sequences is impossible. Also, note that the original form of these two sequences are completely different, so their corresponding approximations are different such that the approximation corresponding to the orthogonal base
\left\{{{T_{j,p}}(Z)}\right\}_{j=0}^{n}
is optimized in the sense of ordinary least squares while the approximation corresponding to the uncorrelated base
\left\{{{X_{j}}}\right\}_{j=0}^{n}
is optimized in the sense of least p-variances.

Referring to (
4.5
) and (
4.6
), let us now consider the following orthogonal approximation sum for the same variable
W_{n}
as stated in (
4.2
),

(4.7)

{S_{n}}=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{\frac{{E\left({{W_{n}}\,{T_{j,p}}(Z)}\right)}}{{E\left({T_{j,p}^{2}(Z)}\right)}}\,{T_{j,p}}(Z)}\,.

Since in (
4.7
),

E\left({{W_{n}}\,{T_{j,p}}(Z)}\right)=E\left({{W_{n}}\,\left({{X_{j}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)}\right)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}},{W_{n}};Z),

and

E\left({T_{j,p}^{2}(Z)}\right)=E\left({{{\left({{X_{j}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)}^{2}}}\right)={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z),

the sum is simplified as

(4.8)

\displaystyle{S_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}},{W_{n}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z)}}\,\left({{X_{j}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)}\,

\displaystyle={W_{n}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{Z}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}},{W_{n}};Z)\,E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z)}}}.

The main point is here: If in (
4.8
),
p=0
or
\left\{{E({X_{j}}Z)=0}\right\}_{j=0}^{n}
(see section
2.3.1
) and/or the limit condition

(4.9)

\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}},{W_{n}};Z)\,E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z)}}}=0,

is satisfied, then
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n\to\infty}\,\,{S_{n}}=\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n\to\infty}\,\,{W_{n}}={W^{*}}
and subsequently

(4.10)

\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}},{W^{*}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{j}};Z)}}{X_{j}}}\,=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}{\frac{{E\left({{W^{*}}{T_{j,p}}(Z)}\right)}}{{E\left({T_{j,p}^{2}(Z)}\right)}}\,{T_{j,p}}(Z)}\,.

This means that the two aforesaid expansions in (
4.10
) coincide each other only if the condition (
4.9
) holds. However, in many cases, this condition will not occur independently as
W_{n}
is distinct for each choice especially in continuous spaces, see corollary
9.2
in this regard. We finally add in a general case that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{{C_{j}}\,{T_{j,p}}(Z)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{{C_{j}}\,\left({{X_{j}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{E({X_{j}}Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{{C_{j}}{X_{j}}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{Z}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,E\left({Z\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{{C_{j}}{X_{j}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{{C_{j}}{X_{j}}}-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\,{\rm{pro}}{{\rm{j}}_{\,Z}}\left({\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}{{C_{j}}{X_{j}}}}\right)\,.

4.1.
A biorthogonality property

Let
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}
be a sequence of complete uncorrelated variables satisfying the condition

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{1}}\,({X_{k}},{X_{j}};Z)={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\,({X_{j}};Z)\,{\delta_{k,j}}.

Since

E\left({{X_{k}}\left({{X_{j}}-\frac{{E({X_{j}}\,Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)}\right)={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{1}}\,({X_{k}},{X_{j}};Z),

so the relation

E\left({{X_{k}}\left({{X_{j}}-\frac{{E({X_{j}}\,Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right)}\right)={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{1}}\,({X_{j}};Z)\,{\delta_{k,j}}.

implies that the sequence
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}
is biorthogonal with respect to the sequence
{\left\{{{X_{j}}-\frac{{E({X_{j}}\,Z)}}{{E({Z^{2}})}}\,Z}\right\}_{j=0}}
. This means that every complete uncorrelated sequence can be biorthogonal but its reverse proposition is not true. See also corollary
8.1
. To study the topic of biorthogonality we refer the readers to
[
3
,
8
]

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}
be p-uncorrelated variables satisfying the condition (4.4) and let
Y
be arbitrary. Then

(4.11)

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{\alpha_{k}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right),

for any selection of constants
\{{\alpha_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
.

Proof.

According to (
1.9
), we first have

(4.12)

\displaystyle{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{\alpha_{k}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y\,;Z}\right)+{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{\alpha_{k}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)-2\,{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{\alpha_{k}}{X_{k}}},Y\,;Z}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y\,;Z}\right)+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\alpha_{k}^{2}\,{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,\left({\,{X_{k}};Z}\right)}-2\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{\alpha_{k}}{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,\left({{X_{k}},Y\,;Z}\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y\,;Z}\right)+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{{\left({{\alpha_{k}}\sqrt{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,\left({\,{X_{k}};Z}\right)}-\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{\sqrt{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,\left({\,{X_{k}};Z}\right)}}}}\right)}^{2}}}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}},

in which the following identity has been used

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{a_{k}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{a_{k}^{2}\,{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,\left({\,{X_{k}};Z}\right)}\Leftrightarrow{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({{X_{i}},{X_{j}}\,;Z}\right)=0\,\,\,{\rm{for}}\,\,i\neq j.

Since
{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y\,;Z}\right)-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}}
is independent of
\{{\alpha_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
in the last equality of (
4.12
), the minimum of
{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{\alpha_{k}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)
is therefore achieved only when

(4.13)

{\alpha_{k}}\sqrt{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,\left({\,{X_{k}};Z}\right)}-\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{\sqrt{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,\left({\,{X_{k}};Z}\right)}}}=0,

which results in the left side of inequality (
4.11
).
∎

With reference to (
1.8
), inequality (
4.11
) can be completed as follows

(4.14)

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{\alpha_{k}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)\leq E\,\left({{{\Big(Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{\alpha_{k}}{X_{k}}}\Big)^{2}}}}\right),

where
\{{\alpha_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
are arbitrary.

Again, inequality (
4.14
) shows the superiority of p-uncorrelated approximations (
3.2
) with respect to any approximation that is made by the least square method (in particular orthogonal approximations). See example
13.1
in this regard.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
be an independent set of random variables.

The problem of finding that linear combination of
{V_{0}},{V_{1}},...,{V_{n}}
which minimizes

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{{\beta_{k}}{V_{k}}}\,;Z}\right),

for any selection of constants
\{{\beta_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
is solved by
\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}
where
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
are mutually p-uncorrelated with respect to the variable
Z
. This corollary tells us that every least p-variance problem with respect to a fixed variable is solved by an appropriate truncated p-uncorrelated expansion of type (
4.1
).

Corollary 4.5
.

Substituting (
4.13
) into (
4.12
) gives

(4.15)

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(Y;Z)-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}}\\
={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(Y;Z)\left({1-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\rho_{p}^{2}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}\right).

On the other side, the positivity of a p-variance implies that

\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\rho_{p}^{2}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}\leq 1,

where
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
satisfy (
4.4
). Also, if
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
is an infinite sequence of p-uncorrelated variables, then the latter inequality changes to

(4.16)

\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}{\rho_{p}^{2}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}\leq 1\,\,\,\,{\rm{or}}\,\,\,\,\,\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}}\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(Y;Z).

Finally, the convergence of the series in (
4.16
) causes that

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{k\to\infty}\,\,\rho_{p}^{2}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)=0\,\,\,\,\,\,{\rm{or}}\,\,\,\,\,\mathop{\lim}\limits_{k\to\infty}\,\,\frac{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}=0\,.

Definition 4.6
.

Convergence in p-variance with respect to a fixed variable

Reconsider the approximation (
2.1
) and constitute the p-variance of its error just like relation (
2.3
). If

(4.17)

\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n\to\infty}\,\,{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({R({c_{0}},...,{c_{n}});Z}\right)=0,

we call the limit relation (
4.17
) “Convergence in p-variance with respect to the fixed variable
Z
”. In this sense, by noting the identity (
4.15
) if

0=\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n\to\infty}\,\,{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)\\
\,\,\,\,={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(Y;Z)\,\,\mathop{\lim}\limits_{n\to\infty}\left({1-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\rho_{p}^{2}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}\right),

then

\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}{\rho_{p}^{2}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}=1\,\,\,\,\,{\rm{or}}\,\,\,\,\,\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}^{2}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}}={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,(Y;Z)\,,

provided that
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}
are mutually p-uncorrelated.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
be linearly independent variables and
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
be their corresponding p-uncorrelated elements generated by
\{{V_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
in theorem
3.2
. Then, for any selection of constants
\{{\lambda_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n-1}
we have

(4.18)

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({{X_{n}}\,;Z}\right)\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({{V_{n}}+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}{{\lambda_{k}}{V_{k}}}\,;Z}\right).

Proof.

First suppose in (
4.11
) that
n\to n-1
and then take
Y=V_{n}
. Noting the last equality of (
3.4
) gives

(4.19)

{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({{V_{n}}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},{V_{n}};Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right)={{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({{X_{n}}\,;Z}\right)\leq{{\mathop{\rm var}}_{p}}\,\left({{V_{n}}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}{{\alpha_{k}}{X_{k}}}\,;Z}\right).

On the other hand, according to relations (
3.3
), there exists an arbitrary sequence, say
\{{\lambda_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n-1}
, such that
-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}{{\alpha_{k}}{X_{k}}}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1}{{\lambda_{k}}{V_{k}}}
in (
4.19
) which completes the proof of (
4.18
).
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\{{X_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{n}
be p-uncorrelated variables with respect to the fixed variable
Z
. Then, for any variable
Y
and every
j=0,1,\ldots,n
we have

{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({Y-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{X_{k}}},{X_{j}}\,;Z}\right)\\
={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({Y,{X_{j}}\,;Z}\right)-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}{\frac{{{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}},Y;Z)}}{{{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}\,({X_{k}};Z)}}{{{\mathop{\rm cov}}}_{p}}\left({{X_{k}},{X_{j}}\,;Z}\right)}\\
={{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({Y,{X_{j}}\,;Z}\right)-{{\mathop{\rm cov}}_{p}}\,\left({{X_{j}},Y\,;Z}\right)=0.

It is now a good position to practically enter the p-uncorrelatedness topic and introduce a sequence of functions in continuous and discrete spaces.

5.
p-uncorrelated functions with respect to a fixed function

Let
\left\{{{\Phi_{k}}(x)}\right\}_{k=0}
be a sequence of continuous functions defined on
[a,b]
and
w(x)
be positive on this interval. Also let
z(x)
be a continuous function such that
\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}>0
. Referring to (
2.30
), we say that
\left\{{{\Phi_{k}}(x)}\right\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
are (weighted) p-uncorrelated functions with respect to the fixed function
z(x)
if they satisfy the condition

(5.1)

\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,dx}-p\frac{{\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}}}{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}}\\
=\left({\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,\Phi_{n}^{2}(x)\,dx}-p\frac{{{{(\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx})}^{2}}\,}}{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}}}\right)\,{\delta_{m,n}}.

Especially when
w(x)z(x)=v(x)
, (
5.1
) becomes

\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,dx}-\lambda_{p}^{2}\int_{\,a}^{b}{v(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,dx}\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{v(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,dx}\\
=\left({\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,\Phi_{n}^{2}(x)\,dx}-\lambda_{p}^{2}{{\Big(\int_{\,a}^{b}{v(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,dx}\Big)^{2}}}}\right)\,{\delta_{m,n}},

where
\lambda_{p}^{2}=p/\int_{\,a}^{b}{\,\frac{{{v^{2}}(x)}}{{w(x)}}\,dx}
(e.g. see relation (
9.9
)).

Noting (
2.31
), such a definition in (
5.1
) can similarly be considered for discrete functions as

\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x){\Phi_{m}}(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)}-p\frac{{\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x){\Phi_{m}}(x)\,z(x)}\,\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x){\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)}}}{{\,\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)}}}\\
=\left({\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x)\Phi_{n}^{2}(x)\,}-p\frac{{{{(\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x){\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)})}^{2}}\,}}{{\,\sum\limits_{x\in{A^{*}}}{j(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)}}}}\right)\,{\delta_{m,n}}\,.

Remark
5.1
.

Relation (
5.1
) clarifies that, after deriving p-uncorrelated functions, one will be able to define a sequence of orthogonal functions in the form

(5.2)

{\Phi_{n}}(x)-(1-\sqrt{1-p})\frac{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}}}{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}}\,z(x)={G_{n}}(x;p)\,,

having the orthogonality property

\displaystyle\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{G_{m}}(x;p){G_{n}}(x;p)\,dx}

\displaystyle=\left({\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,G_{n}^{2}(x;p)\,dx}}\right)\,{\delta_{m,n}}

\displaystyle=\left({\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,dx}}\right)\Big({{{{\mathop{\rm var}}}_{p}}({\Phi_{n}}(x);\,z(x))}\Big){\delta_{m,n}},

whereas its reverse proposition is not feasible when
\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}\neq 0
or
p\neq 0
in (
5.2
). This means that from orthogonal sequences, one cannot derive p-uncorrelated functions as they are somehow an extension of orthogonal functions for
p=0
, while from p-uncorrelated functions the aforesaid aim is always possible by applying (
5.2
). The correctness of such corollaries has been approved by various illustrative examples in the next sections.

There are essentially two classifications for deriving (weighted) p-uncorrelated functions with respect to a fixed function.

5.1.1. First classification.
If the weight function in (
5.1
) is non-classical, i.e. not belonging to the Pearson distributions family and its symmetric analogue
[
15
,
21
]
, the procedure to derive p-uncorrelated functions will lead to the same as generalized Gram-Schmidt process presented in theorem
3.2
or equivalently in the representation (
3.35
). In this sense, note that there are ten classical sequences of real polynomials
[
21
]
orthogonal with respect to the Pearson distributions family

W\,\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{d,}&e\end{array}}\\
{a,\,\,\,b,\,\,\,c}\end{array}}\right|\,x}\right)=\exp\left({\int{\frac{{dx+e}}{{a{x^{2}}+bx+c}}dx}}\right)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,(a,b,c,d,e\in\mathbb{R}),

and its symmetric analogue
[
15
]

{W^{*}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{r,}&s\\
{p,}&q\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)=\exp\left({\int{\frac{{r\,{x^{2}}+s}}{{x(p{x^{2}}+q)}}}\,dx}\right)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,(p,q,r,s\in\mathbb{R}).

Five of them are infinitely orthogonal with respect to particular cases of the two above-mentioned distributions and five other ones are finitely orthogonal which are limited to some parametric constraints. For other applications of orthogonal polynomials, see e.g.
[
1
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
.The following table shows the main characteristics of these sequences.

Table 1.
Characteristics of ten sequences of classical orthogonal polynomials

{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{-1,}&{0,}&1\end{array}}\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)={(1-x)^{u}}{(1+x)^{v}}

{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{0,}&{1,}&0\end{array}}\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)={x^{u}}\exp(-x)

\text{Infinite},[0,\infty)

{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{1,}&{0,}&0\end{array}}\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)={x^{-u}}\exp(-1/x)

\text{Finite},[0,\infty)

\max\,n<(u-1)/2

{S_{n}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{-2u-2v-2,}&{2u}\\
{-1,}&1\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)

{W^{*}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{-2u-2v,}&{2u}\\
{-1,}&1\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)={x^{2u}}{(1-{x^{2}})^{v}}

\text{Infinite},[-1,1]

u>-1/2\,,\,\,v>-1

{S_{n}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{-2,}&{2u}\\
{0,}&1\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)

{W^{*}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{-2,}&{2u}\\
{0,}&1\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)={x^{2u}}\exp(-{x^{2}})

\text{Infinite},(-\infty,\infty)

u>-1/2

{S_{n}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{-2u-2v+2,}&{-2u}\\
{1,}&1\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)

{W^{*}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{-2u-2v,}&{-2u}\\
{1,}&1\end{array}}\right|\,x}\right)={x^{-2u}}{(1+{x^{2}})^{-v}}

\text{Finite},(-\infty,\infty)

\begin{array}[]{l}\,n<u+v-1/2,\\
u<1/2\,,\,\,v>0\end{array}

{S_{n}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{-2u+2,}&2\\
{1,}&0\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)

{W^{*}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{-2u,}&2\\
{1,}&0\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)={x^{-2u}}{\rm{exp}}(-1/{x^{2}})

\text{Finite},(-\infty,\infty)

\max\,n<u-1/2

where

{S_{n}}\left({\left.{\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{r,}&s\\
{p,}&q\end{array}\,}\right|\,x}\right)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{[n/2]}{\,\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{[n/2]}\\
k\end{array}}\right)\prod\limits_{i=0}^{[n/2]-(k+1)}{\frac{{\left({2i+{{(-1)}^{n+1}}+2\,[n/2]}\right)\,p+r}}{{\left({2i+{{(-1)}^{n+1}}+2}\right)\,\,q+s}}}\,\,{x^{n-2k}}={S_{n}}(x),}

is a four-parametric sequence of symmetric orthogonal polynomials
[
15
]
that satisfies the second order differential equation

{x^{2}}(p{x^{2}}+q)\,{S^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(x)+x(r{x^{2}}+s)\,{S^{\prime}_{n}}(x)-\left({n(r+(n-1)p){x^{2}}+(1-{{(-1)}^{n}})\,s/2}\right)\,{S_{n}}(x)=0.

5.1.2. Second classification.
If the weight function belongs to the family of distributions presented in table
1
, there is an analogue theorem below for p-uncorrelated functions similar to the well-known Sturm-Liouville theorem for orthogonal functions
[
21
]
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
a(x),\,b(x),\,u(x)
and
v(x)
be four given functions continuous on the interval
[a,b]
and
p\in[0,1]
. If

w(x)=u(x)\,\exp\left({\int{\frac{{b(x)-a^{\prime}(x)}}{{a(x)}}dx}}\right),

is a positive function on
[a,b]
and the sequence
\left\{{{\Phi_{n}}(x)}\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
with the corresponding eigenvalues
\{{\lambda_{n}}\}
satisfies an integro-differential equation of the form

(5.3)

a(x)\,\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{n}(x)+b(x)\,\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)+\left({{\lambda_{n}}\,u(x)+v(x)}\right){\Phi_{n}}(x)\\
=p\left({{\lambda_{n}}+{A_{z}}}\right)\,u(x)\,z(x)\frac{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}}}{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}},

such that the function
z(x)
satisfies the equation

(5.4)

\frac{{a(x)\,z^{\prime\prime}(x)+b(x)\,z^{\prime}(x)+v(x)\,z(x)}}{{u(x)\,z(x)}}={A_{z}},

where
A_{z}
is a constant value, and also

(5.5)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\alpha_{1}}\,{\Phi_{n}}(a)+{\beta_{1}}\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(a)=0\,,\\[5.69054pt]
{\alpha_{2}}\,{\Phi_{n}}(b)+{\beta_{2}}\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(b)=0\,,\end{array}

are two initial conditions where
{\alpha_{1}}\,,{\beta_{1}},{\alpha_{2}}
and
{\beta_{2}}
are given constants, then
\left\{{{\Phi_{n}}(x)}\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
are (weighted) p-uncorrelated functions with respect to the fixed function
z(x)
.

Proof.

For the sake of simplicity, we assume in (
5.3
) that
\frac{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}}}{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}}=\beta_{n}^{*}
. Then, equation (
5.3
) can be written in the self-adjoint form

(5.6)

{\left({r(x)\,\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)}\right)^{\prime}}+\left({{\lambda_{n}}\,w(x)+q(x)}\right){\Phi_{n}}(x)=p\left({{\lambda_{n}}+{A_{z}}}\right)\,\beta_{n}^{*}\,w(x)\,z(x),

and for the index
m
as

(5.7)

{\left({r(x)\,\Phi^{\prime}_{m}(x)}\right)^{\prime}}+\left({{\lambda_{m}}\,w(x)+q(x)}\right){\Phi_{m}}(x)=p\left({{\lambda_{m}}+{A_{z}}}\right)\,\beta_{m}^{*}\,w(x)\,z(x),

in which

r(x)=a(x)\,\exp\left({\int{\frac{{b(x)-a^{\prime}(x)}}{{a(x)}}dx}}\right),

and

q(x)=v(x)\,\exp\left({\int{\frac{{b(x)-a^{\prime}(x)}}{{a(x)}}dx}}\right).

On multiplying by
{\Phi_{m}}(x)
and
{\Phi_{n}}(x)
in respectively (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) and subtracting we get

\left[{r(x)\left({{\Phi_{m}}(x)\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)-{\Phi_{n}}(x)\Phi^{\prime}_{m}(x)}\right)}\right]_{\,a}^{\,b}+\left({{\lambda_{n}}-{\lambda_{m}}}\right)\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,dx}\\
=p\left({{\lambda_{n}}+{A_{z}}}\right)\beta_{n}^{*}\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}-p\left({{\lambda_{m}}+{A_{z}}}\right)\beta_{m}^{*}\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}\\
=p\left({{\lambda_{n}}-{\lambda_{m}}}\right)\frac{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}}}{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}},

which results in

\left({{\lambda_{n}}-{\lambda_{m}}}\right)\left({\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,dx}-p\frac{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,z(x)\,dx}}}{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}}}\right)\\
=-\left[{r(x)\,\left({{\Phi_{m}}(x)\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)-{\Phi_{n}}(x)\Phi^{\prime}_{m}(x)}\right)}\right]_{\,a}^{\,b}=0\,\,\,\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,\,\,n\neq m.

∎

It is important to mention that the integro-differential equation (
5.3
) can be transformed to a fourth order equation using (
5.4
). First of all, without loss of generality, we can assume in (
5.3
) that
u(x)=1
, because it is just enough to divide both side of (
5.3
) by
u(x)
. In this case, substituting

z(x)=\frac{1}{{p\left({{\lambda_{n}}+{A_{z}}}\right)\beta_{n}^{*}}}\left({a(x)\,\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{n}(x)+b(x)\,\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)+\left({{\lambda_{n}}\,+v(x)}\right){\Phi_{n}}(x)}\right)

into the equation

a(x)\,z^{\prime\prime}(x)+b(x)\,z^{\prime}(x)+\left({v(x)-{A_{z}}}\right)z(x)=0,

yields

{a^{2}}(x)\,\Phi_{n}^{(4)}(x)+2a(x)\left({a^{\prime}(x)+b(x)}\right)\Phi_{n}^{(3)}(x)+\\
\left({a(x)\left({a^{\prime\prime}(x)+2b^{\prime}(x)+2v(x)+{\lambda_{n}}-{A_{z}}}\right)+b(x)\left({a^{\prime}(x)+b(x)}\right)}\right)\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{n}(x)\\
+\left({a(x)\left({b^{\prime\prime}(x)+2v^{\prime}(x)}\right)+b(x)\left({b^{\prime}(x)+2v(x)+{\lambda_{n}}-{A_{z}}}\right)}\right)\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)\\
+\left({a(x)v^{\prime\prime}(x)+b(x)v^{\prime}(x)+{v^{2}}(x)+({\lambda_{n}}-{A_{z}})v(x)-{A_{z}}{\lambda_{n}}}\right){\Phi_{n}}(x)=0,

having four independent basis solutions.

5.2.1. An extension of the previous theorem.
This turn, assume in the right side of (
5.3
) that the function
u(x)z(x)=h(x)
satisifies a second order differential equation of the form

p(x)\,h^{\prime\prime}(x)+q(x)\,h^{\prime}(x)+r(x)\,h(x)=0,

instead of satisfying the equation (
5.4
) where
p(x),q(x)
and
r(x)
are all known functions. Then, equation (
5.3
) will be transformed to the fourth order equation

p(x){\left({a(x)\,\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{n}(x)+b(x)\,\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)+\left({{\lambda_{n}}\,u(x)+v(x)}\right){\Phi_{n}}(x)}\right)^{\prime\prime}}\\
+q(x)\,{\left({a(x)\,\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{n}(x)+b(x)\,\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)+\left({{\lambda_{n}}\,u(x)+v(x)}\right){\Phi_{n}}(x)}\right)^{\prime}}\\
+r(x)\,\left({a(x)\,\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{n}(x)+b(x)\,\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)+\left({{\lambda_{n}}\,u(x)+v(x)}\right){\Phi_{n}}(x)}\right)=0,

which is equivalent to

(5.8)

p(x)a(x)\,\Phi_{n}^{(4)}(x)+\Big({p(x)\left({2a^{\prime}(x)+b(x)}\right)+q(x)\,a(x)}\Big)\Phi_{n}^{(3)}(x)\\
+\Big({p(x)u(x){\lambda_{n}}+p(x)\left({a^{\prime\prime}(x)+2b^{\prime}(x)+v(x)}\right)+q(x)\left({a^{\prime}(x)+b(x)}\right)+r(x)a(x)}\Big)\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{n}(x)\\
+\Big({\left({2p(x)u^{\prime}(x)+q(x)u(x)}\right){\lambda_{n}}+p(x)\left({b^{\prime\prime}(x)+2v^{\prime}(x)}\right)+q(x)\left({b^{\prime}(x)+v(x)}\right)+r(x)b(x)}\Big)\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)\\
+\Big({\left({p(x)u^{\prime\prime}(x)+q(x)u^{\prime}(x)+r(x)u(x)}\right){\lambda_{n}}+p(x)v^{\prime\prime}(x)+q(x)v^{\prime}(x)+r(x)v(x)}\Big){\Phi_{n}}(x)=0.

Noting the above-mentioned comments, equation (
5.3
) can now be generalized as

a(x)\,{\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(x)+b(x)\,{\Phi^{\prime}_{n}}(x)+\left({{\lambda_{n}}\,u(x)+v(x)}\right)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)={R_{n}}(x),

whose self-adjoint form

{\left({r(x)\,\Phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)}\right)^{\prime}}+\left({{\lambda_{n}}\,w(x)+q(x)}\right)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)=\frac{{w(x)}}{{u(x)}}\,{R_{n}}(x),

yields

(5.9)

\left[{r(x)\,\left({{\Phi_{m}}(x){\Phi^{\prime}_{n}}(x)-{\Phi_{n}}(x){\Phi^{\prime}_{m}}(x)}\right)}\right]_{\,a}^{\,b}+\left({{\lambda_{n}}-{\lambda_{m}}}\right)\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)\,dx}\\
=\int_{\,a}^{b}{\frac{{w(x)}}{{u(x)}}\,\left({{R_{n}}(x)\,{\Phi_{m}}(x)-{R_{m}}(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)}\right)\,dx}\,.

Now, if in (
5.9
) we set

{R_{n}}(x)=p\left({{\lambda_{n}}+{A_{z}}}\right)\frac{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,z(x)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)\,dx}}}{{\,\int_{\,a}^{b}{w(x)\,{z^{2}}(x)\,dx}}}u(x)\,z(x),

we directly get to theorem
5.2
. See also section
9.1.1
and relations (
9.29
) to (
9.30
).

5.2.2. Remark.
The previous section shows that equation (
5.3
) is in fact a non-homogenous second order differential equation whose general solution for
p=1
reads as

(5.10)

{\Phi_{n}}(x)={c_{1}}{y_{1,n}}(x)+{c_{2}}{y_{2,n}}(x)+{\lambda^{*}}z(x)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,({c_{1}},{c_{2}},{\lambda^{*}}\in\mathbb{R}),

where
{c_{1}}{y_{1,n}}(x)+{c_{2}}{y_{2,n}}(x)
is the solution of the homogenous equation

a(x)\,{\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(x)+b(x)\,{\Phi^{\prime}_{n}}(x)+\left({{\lambda_{n}}\,p(x)+q(x)}\right)\,{\Phi_{n}}(x)=0,

with two independent solutions
{y_{1,n}}(x)
and
{y_{2,n}}(x)
